Why Does Agentic Safety Fail to Generalize Across Tasks?
Yonatan Slutzky Yotam Alexander Tomer Slor Tel Aviv University Tel Aviv University Tel Aviv University slutzky1@mail.tau.ac.il yotam.alexander@gmail.com tomerslor@gmail.com and Yoav Nagel Nadav Cohen Tel Aviv University Tel Aviv University yoavyosefn@mail.tau.ac.il cohennadav@tauex.tau.ac.il

Abstract
AI agents are increasingly deployed in multi-task settings, where the task to perform is specified at test time, and the agent must generalize to unseen tasks. A major concern in such settings is safety: often, an agent must not only execute unseen tasks, but do so while avoiding risks and handling ones that materialize. Empirical evidence suggests that even when the ability to execute generalizes to unseen tasks, the ability to do so safely frequently does not. This paper provides theory and experiments indicating that failures of agentic safety to generalize across tasks are not merely due to limitations of training methods, but reflect an inherent property of safety itself: the relationship between a task and its safe execution is more complex than the relationship between a task and its execution alone. Theoretically, we analyze linear-quadratic control with H ∞ H_{\infty} -robustness, and prove that the mapping from task specification to an optimal controller has higher Lipschitz constant with safety requirements than without, yielding a Lipschitz bound of independent interest. Empirically, we demonstrate our conclusions in simulated quadcopter navigation with a neural network agent and in CRM with an LLM agent. Our findings suggest that current efforts to enhance agentic safety may be insufficient, and point to a need for fundamentally different approaches.



1
Introduction

The evolution of Artificial Intelligence (AI) is manifested by its progression from passive settings, where it processes static data for generating predictions, to
agentic
settings, where it interacts with dynamic environments for performing tasks entailing long-term rewards.
Agentic settings have been studied for decades in the context of Reinforcement Learning (RL)
[
53
,
101
,
64
,
15
]
and control theory
[
95
,
76
,
127
,
7
]
, and in recent years—due to the rapid proliferation of Large Language Models (LLMs)
[
20
,
104
,
81
,
21
,
16
,
25
,
66
]
—they have become a central facet of practical AI
[
120
,
96
,
128
,
30
,
67
,
74
,
45
]
.
Unlike classic RL and control theory formulations, which typically consider a single (fixed) task, modern agentic AI is predominantly
multi-task
: there are multiple possible tasks, and the one to perform is specified only post-deployment (
i.e.
, at test time)
[
28
,
35
,
113
,
37
,
91
,
130
,
123
,
131
,
59
]
.
Often, it is impractical to train on all possible tasks, and the hope is that a learned agent will
generalize across tasks
, meaning it will perform tasks unseen in training as effectively as it performs tasks that are seen in training.

A primary concern in agentic AI is
safety
: when decisions are made and acted upon with no human in the loop, the implications of unsafe behavior can be catastrophic
[
11
,
52
,
118
,
36
,
4
]
.
For example, an autonomous navigation agent may lead a vehicle to a high risk region (
e.g.
, one with many obstacles), and a customer-facing agent may succumb to phishing attacks, revealing sensitive enterprise information.
Two important characteristics of agentic safety manifested in the latter two examples are:

(i)

risk avoidance
: a preference for avoiding situations of high risk;
and

(ii)

risk handling
: an ability to cope with risks when they materialize.

In many applications it is difficult to formulate criteria that ensure agentic safety
[
51
,
40
,
17
]
.
A common approach for circumventing said difficulty is to incorporate safety into a learned agent through
imitation learning
,
i.e.
, through demonstrations from a trusted safe teacher (often a human)
[
8
,
84
,
26
,
103
]
.
While this approach has been shown to enhance safe behavior on tasks included in training
[
69
,
94
]
, the
safe behavior often fails to generalize across tasks
, even in cases where the agent’s ability to execute does generalize
[
94
,
78
,
6
,
129
,
61
]
.
This phenomenon raises a fundamental question: are failures of agentic safety to generalize across tasks caused by limitations in current training techniques, or are they due to inherent properties that make safety less transferable across tasks than the ability to execute?

In this paper, we theoretically and empirically investigate the foregoing question.
Theoretically, we analyze what is perhaps the simplest embodiment of learning a multi-task agent with safety requirements: learning to control a Linear Dynamical System (LDS) to maximize a task-dependent quadratic reward, while handling a risk of potential disturbances,
i.e.
, while ensuring robustness in the sense of
H_{\infty}
norm
[
95
,
76
,
127
,
5
,
13
,
41
]
.
We prove that the mapping from task specification (quadratic reward matrix) to an optimal controller has higher Lipschitz constant under safety requirements (
i.e.
, with
H_{\infty}
-robustness) than it does when safety is ignored (
i.e.
, without
H_{\infty}
-robustness).
In light of the literature
[
19
,
12
,
46
,
108
,
115
,
121
,
89
,
97
]
tying the Lipschitz constant of a target mapping to the difficulty of generalizing when learning it, we conclude that
generalization across tasks is fundamentally more difficult with safety requirements than without
.
This holds
regardless of how well safety requirements are met on tasks seen in training
.
As a technical contribution of independent interest, we establish an upper bound for the Lipschitz constant of the Linear-Quadratic Regulator (LQR) as a function of its state cost matrix
Q

[
32
,
5
]
.

We corroborate our theory via experiments in three settings that span different types of agents and different notions of safety:

(i)
a setting that mirrors our theoretical analysis: learning to control an LDS to maximize a task-dependent quadratic reward while handling a risk of potential disturbances;

(ii)
a setting in which a neural network agent is trained to navigate a simulated quadcopter to a task-dependent location, while avoiding regions marked as high-risk;
and

(iii)
a setting in which an LLM agent (LLaMA-3.2 model
[
73
]
) is fine-tuned to perform tasks in a realistic Customer Relationship Management (CRM) benchmark
[
63
]
, while avoiding situations marked as high-risk and handling events defined as a risk materializing.
Our experiments compare learning to imitate a safe teacher to learning to imitate an unsafe teacher (
i.e.
, a teacher that executes tasks while ignoring safety).
In all settings, we find that although imitating safe and unsafe teachers on tasks seen in training is similarly straightforward, generalizing across tasks is considerably more difficult with the safe teacher.

Overall, our findings suggest that failures of agentic safety to generalize across tasks are at least in part due to inherent properties of safety, and more specifically, to a complex relationship between the task to be executed and the characteristics of safe execution.
From a practical standpoint, this implies that current efforts to enhance agentic safety through more training on a subset of tasks may be insufficient, necessitating a fundamentally different approach.
A promising possibility suggested by our findings is to learn representations for task specifications and safety characteristics which simplify the relationship between them.
We hope that our work will spur progress along this line.

1.1
Paper Organization

The remainder of the paper is organized as follows.

Section

2
presents a formalism for reasoning about learning multi-task agents with safety requirements.

Section

3
delivers our theoretical analysis.

Section

4
reports our experimental results.

Section

5
discusses limitations of our work.

Section

6
surveys related work.
Finally,
Section

7
concludes.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notation

We use non-boldface lowercase letters for denoting scalars (
e.g.
,
\alpha\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
), boldface lowercase letters for denoting vectors (
e.g.
,
{\mathbf{x}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
), and non-boldface uppercase letters for denoting matrices (
e.g.
,
A\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d,d}
).
For
d\in{\mathbb{N}}
, we let
[d]:=\{1,\ldots,d\}
.
We respectively use
{\mathbf{e}}_{j}
and
I
to denote the
j
-th standard basis vector and the identity matrix, with their dimensions omitted from the notation and inferred from context.
We use
\|\cdot\|_{2}
to denote the Euclidean norm for vectors and the induced spectral norm for matrices, and
\|\cdot\|_{F}
to denote the Frobenius norm for matrices.
For a square matrix
P
, we use
\Tr(P)
to denote its trace, and write
P\succ 0
(respectively,
P\succeq 0
) to indicate that
P
is positive definite (respectively, positive semidefinite).
For
d\in\mathbb{N}
and a function
f:{\mathcal{V}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d,d}
over some domain
{\mathcal{V}}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d,d}
, we use
\text{Lip}(f)
to denote the Lipschitz constant of
f(\cdot)
, defined by
\text{Lip}(f):=\sup_{V_{1},V_{2}\in{\mathcal{V}},V_{1}\neq V_{2}}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{f(V_{1})-f(V_{2})}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{F}\big/\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{V_{1}-V_{2}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{F}
.

2.2
Learning a Multi-Task Agent with Safety Requirements

We consider the below formulation of learning a multi-task agent with safety requirements.
The formulation captures not only conventional multi-task settings in RL and control theory, but also contemporary settings involving LLM agents; see
Section

4.3
for details.

An
environment
comprises a space of
states

{\mathcal{X}}
, a space of
observations

{\mathcal{O}}
, a space of
actions

{\mathcal{U}}
, and a space of
tasks

\Theta
.
At every time
t\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
, the environment holds a state
{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{X}}
, produces an observation
{\mathbf{o}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{O}}
, intakes an action
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{U}}
, and grants a reward
r_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
, where the latter depends on a chosen task
\theta\in\Theta
.
The state evolves per the update rule
{\mathbf{x}}_{t+1}=f_{\text{state}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{t},t)
, where
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
is the environment’s
state transition function
, and the initial state
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
is set by some (deterministic or random) assignment.
The observation is produced per
{\mathbf{o}}_{t}=f_{\text{obs}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},t)
, where
f_{\text{obs}}(\cdot)
is the environment’s
observation function
.
The reward is assigned per
r_{t}=f_{\text{reward}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},{\mathbf{o}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{t},t;\theta)
, where
f_{\text{reward}}(\cdot)
is the environment’s
reward function
.
1
1

1

It is possible to account for environments where the reward depends on states, observations and actions from the past (
i.e.
, where
f_{\text{reward}}(\cdot)
respectively intakes
({\mathbf{x}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t}
,
({\mathbf{o}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t}
and
({\mathbf{u}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t}
in place of
{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
,
{\mathbf{o}}_{t}
and
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
), but for simplicity we consider the typical setting of an instantaneous reward.

Each of the functions
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
,
f_{\text{obs}}(\cdot)
and
f_{\text{reward}}(\cdot)
may be stochastic (in the sense that its output may be sampled from some random distribution), and is allowed to vary with time (hence it receives the time index as an input argument).

An
agent
interacts with the environment by assigning, at every time
t\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
, the action
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
based on: the concurrent observation
{\mathbf{o}}_{t}
, the past observations
({\mathbf{o}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t-1}
, the past actions
({\mathbf{u}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t-1}
, and the chosen task
\theta
.
Namely, the agent implements
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=\pi_{\phi}({\mathbf{o}}_{t},({\mathbf{o}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t-1},({\mathbf{u}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t-1};\theta)
, where
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot)
is the agent’s
policy function
, which may be stochastic, and is parameterized by
\phi\in\Phi
.
The ability of the agent to execute the task
\theta
is quantified by the
expected cumulative reward

{\mathcal{R}}(\phi;\theta):=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\big[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}r_{t}\big]
, where the expectation is over the (possible) randomness in the environment (state transition function
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
, observation function
f_{\text{obs}}(\cdot)
, reward function
f_{\text{reward}}(\cdot)
, and the assignment of the initial state
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
) and in the agent (policy function
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot)
).

The extent to which the agent executes a task safely is evaluated through the complementary notions of
risk avoidance
and
risk handling
.
Risk avoidance is formalized by defining a set of banned states
{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{ban}}\subset{\mathcal{X}}
known to the agent, and requiring the agent to avoid those,
i.e.
, to assign actions such that
{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\notin{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{ban}}
for every
t\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\}
.
This corresponds to agentic settings with known “no-go” regions, for example autonomous navigation around restricted areas, robotic manipulation near fragile objects, and industrial control systems with hard operating limits.
Risk handling is formalized by introducing disturbances to state transitions and requiring the agent to mitigate degradation in the expected cumulative reward.
Specifically, the requirement is that the agent assign actions such that replacement of the state transition function
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
with any function from a set
{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dist}}
does not lead to a significant reduction in
{\mathcal{R}}(\cdot)
.
This captures agentic settings where risks cannot be fully avoided and must be dealt with as they materialize, for example autonomous driving under sudden wind gusts or slippery roads, LLM agents exposed to phishing attacks, and industrial control systems encountering never-before-seen regimes.

The agent’s policy function
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot)
is trained via
imitation learning
from a
teacher policy function

\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot)
over a set of
training tasks

\Theta_{\mathrm{train}}\subset\Theta
.
That is, the agent’s parameters
\phi
are tuned with the objective of having
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\,;\theta)
match
\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot\,;\theta)
for every
\theta\in\Theta_{\mathrm{train}}
.

Generalization across tasks
is measured by the extent to which
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\,;\theta)
matches
\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot\,;\theta)
when the task
\theta
is unseen in training,
i.e.
, when
\theta\in\Theta_{\mathrm{test}}:=\Theta\setminus\Theta_{\mathrm{train}}
.
In practical settings,
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot)
is trained on a finite sample,
i.e.
, on finitely many input-output examples extracted from observation-action trajectories that are generated by applying
\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot;\theta)
to the environment, where the task
\theta
ranges over
\Theta_{\mathrm{train}}
.
In theoretical settings, to decouple finite sample effects from the study of generalization across tasks, one may consider training
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot)
in the infinite sample limit, meaning
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\,;\theta)=\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot\,;\theta)
for every
\theta\in\Theta_{\mathrm{train}}
.

Our interest lies in comparing different scenarios in terms of generalization across tasks.
Specifically, we compare scenarios where the teacher policy function
\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot)
is safe (in terms of risk avoidance and/or risk handling), against ones where
\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot)
is unsafe (
i.e.
, it executes tasks while ignoring safety).
Since safety requirements can significantly alter attainable rewards, a reward-based comparison may be misleading.
Instead, we base the comparison on
imitation error
,
i.e.
, on the distance between the outputs of
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot\,;\theta)
and
\pi_{\phi^{*}}(\cdot\,;\theta)
for identical inputs, where
\theta\in\Theta_{\mathrm{test}}
.

2.3
Special Case: Linear-Quadratic Control with
H_{\infty}
-Robustness

Below we describe what is perhaps the simplest special case of the formulation in
Section

2.2
: learning to control a
Linear Dynamical System
(
LDS
) to maximize a task-dependent quadratic reward, while ensuring
H_{\infty}
-robustness

[
95
,
76
,
127
,
5
,
13
,
41
]
.

For some
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
, let the state, observation and action spaces,
i.e.
,
{\mathcal{X}}
,
{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{U}}
, respectively, all equal
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
.
Let the state transition function
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
be given by:

\displaystyle f_{\text{state}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{t},t)=A{\mathbf{x}}_{t}+B{\mathbf{u}}_{t}+D{\mathbf{w}}_{t}\text{\,,}

(1)

where:

(i)

A,B,D\in\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
are referred to as the
state matrix
, the
input matrix
and the
noise matrix
, respectively;

(ii)

({\mathbf{w}}_{t})_{t=0}^{\infty}
are independent random variables over
{\mathbb{R}}^{m}
whose means are zero and whose variances are summable, meaning
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\big[\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{{\mathbf{w}}_{t}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}^{2}\big]\leq c
for some constant
c\in{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
;
2
2

2

Summability of variances is required in order to ensure that the expected cumulative reward
{\mathcal{R}}(\cdot)
is finite; see
Section

A.1
for details.

and

(iii)
the initial state
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}
is zero.
Assume stability and non-degeneracy, in the sense that
\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{A}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\in(0,1)
and
\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{B}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\neq 0
.
Consider full observability, meaning that observations are equal to their concurrent states,
i.e.
, the observation function
f_{\text{obs}}(\cdot)
is given by
f_{\text{obs}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},t)={\mathbf{x}}_{t}
.
Let the task space
\Theta
equal
\{Q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m,m}:Q\succeq 0\text{ and }\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{Q}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{F}\leq 1\}
, and the reward function
f_{\text{reward}}(\cdot)
be given by
f_{\text{reward}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{t},t;Q)=-{\mathbf{x}}_{t}^{\top}Q{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-{\mathbf{u}}_{t}^{\top}R{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
, where
Q
is referred to as the
state reward matrix
, and
R\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m,m}
satisfies
R\succ 0
and is referred to as the
action reward matrix
.

Consider the risk handling notion of safety, where
{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dist}}
consists of all deterministic analogues of
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
,
i.e.
, of all functions that adhere to
Equation

1
and the subsequent text, except that they assign
({\mathbf{w}}_{t})_{t=0}^{\infty}
deterministically, to values which may not be zero but uphold
\sum_{t=0}^{\infty}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{{\mathbf{w}}_{t}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}^{2}\leq c
.
The requirement from the agent to mitigate degradation in the expected cumulative reward
{\mathcal{R}}(\cdot)
is formalized through a worst-case lens.
Namely, for a given task
Q\in\Theta
, the agent is required to maximize
\inf{\mathcal{R}}(\cdot\,;Q)
, where the infimum is taken over all cases where
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
is replaced by a member of
{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dist}}
.
This is known in the control theory literature as
H_{\infty}
-robustness
[
127
,
13
,
41
]
.

A fundamental result in control theory states that in the absence of safety requirements, the optimal policy function is the
Linear-Quadratic Regulator
(
LQR
)
[
127
,
13
,
41
]
.
Specifically, with
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
maintaining its original form (
Equation

1
and subsequent text), for a given task
Q\in\Theta
, the expected cumulative reward
{\mathcal{R}}(\phi;Q)
attains its maximal possible value if the agent’s parameters
\phi
are such that actions are assigned as
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(Q){\mathbf{o}}_{t}
, where
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(Q)\in\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
is referred to as the
LQR whose state cost matrix is
Q
(see
Section

A.1
for further details).
Control theory also provides, for any
Q\in\Theta
, a canonical construction for a linear policy function that is optimal under safety requirements
[
127
,
13
,
41
]
.
More precisely, the theory of
H_{\infty}
-robustness provides a canonical mapping
Q\mapsto K_{\mathrm{safe}}(Q)\in\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
such that the following holds: for a given task
Q\in\Theta
, when
f_{\text{state}}(\cdot)
can be replaced by any
f_{\text{dist}}(\cdot)\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dist}}
, if
\phi
are such that actions are assigned as
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=K_{\mathrm{safe}}(Q){\mathbf{o}}_{t}
, then
\inf_{f_{\text{dist}}(\cdot)\in{\mathcal{F}}_{\text{dist}}}{\mathcal{R}}(\phi;Q)
attains its maximal possible value (see
Section

A.2
for further details).

In accordance with the optimal unsafe and safe policy functions, consider the following form for the agent’s policy function:
\pi_{\phi}({\mathbf{o}}_{t},({\mathbf{o}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t-1},({\mathbf{u}}_{\tau})_{\tau=0}^{t-1};Q)=K_{\phi}(Q){\mathbf{o}}_{t}
, where
K_{\phi}:\Theta\to\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
is a mapping parameterized by
\phi
, which realizes
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
and
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
for some parameter values
\phi_{\mathrm{unsafe}}
and
\phi_{\mathrm{safe}}
, respectively.
We compare a scenario of imitation learning where the teacher policy function is
\pi_{\phi_{\mathrm{unsafe}}}(\cdot)
, against one where it is
\pi_{\phi_{\mathrm{safe}}}(\cdot)
.
In the former scenario, training in the infinite sample limit means that
K_{\phi}(Q)
equals
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(Q)
for every
Q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{train}}
, and generalization across tasks is measured by the discrepancy between
K_{\phi}(Q)
and
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(Q)
for
Q\in\Theta_{\mathrm{test}}
.
The same holds in the latter scenario, but with
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(Q)
in place of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(Q)
.

3
Theoretical Analysis

Section

2.3
presents what is perhaps the simplest embodiment of learning a multi-task agent with safety requirements: learning to control an LDS to maximize a task-dependent quadratic reward, while ensuring
H_{\infty}
-robustness.
In the setting of
Section

2.3
, the task space
\Theta
is
\{Q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{m,m}:Q\succeq 0\text{ and }\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{Q}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{F}\leq 1\}
, and generalization across tasks with and without safety requirements reduces to generalization in learning certain target mappings
K_{\mathrm{safe}}:\Theta\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m,m}
and
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}:\Theta\to{\mathbb{R}}^{m,m}
, respectively.
In this section we deliver a theoretical analysis which proves that, under technical conditions, the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is greater than that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
.
In light of the literature
[
19
,
12
,
46
,
108
,
115
,
121
,
89
,
97
]
tying the Lipschitz constant of a target mapping to the difficulty of generalizing when learning it, our theory establishes a formal sense in which
generalization across tasks is fundamentally more difficult with safety requirements than without
.
Given that the analyzed setting (
Section

2.3
) considers training in the infinite sample limit, the established difficulty of safety to generalize across tasks holds
despite safety being learned perfectly on training tasks
.

An intermediate step in our analysis is a derivation of an upper bound for the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
.
In control theory,
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
represents the mapping from a state cost matrix to its corresponding LQR.
Given the importance of this mapping, the bound we derive for its Lipschitz constant is viewed as a technical contribution of independent interest.

3.1
Stability Margin

Our analysis relies on a
stability margin
condition for the LDS, which requires the spectral norm of the state matrix
A
to be bounded away from unity by a certain quantity—see
1
.
While this condition is a technical requirement of our current proof techniques, we demonstrate empirically in
Section

4.1
that our theoretical conclusions persist even when it is violated.
Relaxing the condition is an important direction for future work (see
Section

5
).

Assumption 1

(Stability Margin)
.

The spectral norm of the state matrix
A
satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{A}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}<1-\frac{1}{1+\big(\sqrt{2}+\sqrt{2}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{B}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}^{2}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{R^{-1}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\big)^{-1}}\text{\,.}

(2)

3.2
Upper Bound for the Lipschitz Constant of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)

Lemma

1
establishes an upper bound for the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
.
In control theory,
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
represents the mapping from a state cost matrix to its corresponding LQR.
To our knowledge,
Lemma

1
constitutes the first explicit Lipschitz characterization for
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
.
Given the importance of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
, we view
Lemma

1
as a technical contribution of independent interest.

Lemma 1
.

Under
1
, the Lipschitz constant of the mapping
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
satisfies:

\displaystyle\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{unsafe}})\leq 2\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{A}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{B}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{R^{-1}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\big(1+2\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{B}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}^{2}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{R^{-1}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\big)\text{\,.}

(3)

Proof sketch (full proof in
Appendix

B
).

By a fundamental result in control theory
[
127
,
13
,
41
,
15
]
, for any
Q\in\Theta
, the matrix
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(Q)\in\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
(LQR whose state cost matrix is
Q
) is given by a rational function of
A
,
B
and
R
, as well as a matrix
P(Q)\in\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
that is the unique fixed point of a
Riccati operator
depending on
Q
.
Under
1
, there exists a bounded region in which
P(Q)
must lie for any
Q\in\Theta
, and within which the Riccati operator is a contraction for any
Q\in\Theta
.
This implies that
P(Q)
varies in a controlled manner as
Q
changes, yielding an upper bound for the Lipschitz constant of the mapping
Q\mapsto P(Q)
.

Equation

3
then follows from propagating this upper bound through the rational function that gives
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(Q)
.
∎

3.3
Separation between the Lipschitz Constants of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
and
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)

Theorem

1
proves that the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is greater than or equal to that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
times a particular coefficient which can be arbitrarily large.
This establishes a formal sense in which
generalization across tasks is fundamentally more difficult with safety requirements than without, even when safety requirements are met perfectly on training tasks
(see opening of
Section

3
).

Theorem

1
relies on two conditions in addition to stability margin (
1
).
While these conditions, formulated in
2
and
3
, are technical requirements of our current proof technique, we demonstrate empirically in
Section

4.1
that the conclusions of
Theorem

1
persist even when they are violated.
Relaxing the conditions is an important direction for future work (see
Section

5
).

The first of the two conditions is that all fixed matrices are symmetric and commuting.

Assumption 2

(Symmetric Commutativity)
.

The state, input and noise matrices
A
,
B
and
D
, respectively, are symmetric and commute with one another and with the action reward matrix
R
.

2
implies that the matrices
A
,
B
,
D
and
R
are simultaneously orthogonally diagonalizable,
i.e.
, there exists an orthogonal matrix
V\in\mathbb{R}^{m,m}
such that:

\displaystyle A=V\diag(\lambda_{1}(A),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(A))\,V^{\top}\quad,\quad B=V\diag(\lambda_{1}(B),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(B))\,V^{\top}\,,

(4)

\displaystyle D=V\diag(\lambda_{1}(D),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(D))\,V^{\top}\quad,\quad R=V\diag(\lambda_{1}(R),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(R))\,V^{\top}\,,

where, for
M\in\{A,B,D,R\}
,
\lambda_{1}(M),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(M)
are the eigenvalues of
M
ordered according to the shared eigenbasis.
By the definition of spectral norm:
3
3

3

Recall that
R\succ 0
, implying
\lambda_{1}(R),\ldots,\lambda_{m}(R)>0
.

\displaystyle\max\nolimits_{j\in[m]}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{\lambda_{j}(A)}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}=\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{A}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}~~,~~\max\nolimits_{j\in[m]}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{\lambda_{j}(B)}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rvert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}=\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{B}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}~~,~~\min\nolimits_{j\in[m]}\lambda_{j}(R)=\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{R^{-1}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}^{-1}\text{\,.}

(5)

Definition

1
defines the
alignment factor
: a quantity that measures how close
A
,
B
and
R
are to attaining all extrema in
Equation

5
at a common index
j\in[m]
.

Definition 1

(Alignment Factor)
.

Under
2
and the notation of
Equation

4
, define the
alignment factor

\alpha_{\mathrm{align}}
to be the largest
\alpha\in(0,1]
for which there exists an index
j\in[m]
satisfying:
4
4

4

\alpha_{\mathrm{align}}
is well-defined since
Equation

6
is trivially satisfied for some
j\in[m]
when
\alpha
is sufficiently small.

\displaystyle|\lambda_{j}(A)|\geq\alpha^{-1}\big(\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{A}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}+\alpha-1\big)\quad,\quad|\lambda_{j}(B)|\geq\alpha\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{B}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\quad,\quad\lambda_{j}(R)\leq\alpha^{-1}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{R^{-1}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}^{-1}\text{\,.}

(6)

The final condition required by
Theorem

1
is a mild regularity of the alignment factor (
Definition

1
).

Assumption 3

(Alignment Regularity)
.

Under
2
and the notation of
Equation

4
, among the indices
j\in[m]
that attain
Equation

6
with
\alpha=\alpha_{\mathrm{align}}
, at least one satisfies
\lambda_{j}(A)\neq 0
and
\lambda_{j}(D)\neq 0
.

With
2
and
3
in place (in addition to
1
),
Theorem

1
proves that the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is greater than or equal to that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
times a particular coefficient (
Equation

7
).
While this coefficient can be made arbitrarily large (via appropriate choices of the state, input, noise and action reward matrices
A
,
B
,
D
and
R
, respectively), there are cases where it is smaller than one.
Empirically, however, we find the estimated Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
to consistently be greater than that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
(see
Section

4.1
).
Developing a stronger version of
Theorem

1
in which the coefficient is always greater than one is an important direction for future work (see
Section

5
).

Theorem 1
.

Under
1
,
2
, and
3
, the Lipschitz constants of the mappings
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
and
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
satisfy:

\displaystyle\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{safe}})\geq\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{align}}^{3}}{\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{A}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\big(2+4\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{B}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}^{2}\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{R^{-1}}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}_{2}\big)}\cdot\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{unsafe}})\text{\,.}

(7)

Proof sketch (full proof in
Appendix

C
).

The proof establishes
Equation

7
by deriving a lower bound on
\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{safe}})
and combining that with the upper bound on
\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{unsafe}})
from
Lemma

1
(
Equation

3
).
For the lower bound on
\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{safe}})
, attention is restricted to the subset
\Theta_{V}\subset\Theta
comprising the matrices
Q\in\Theta
that are diagonalized by the orthogonal matrix
V
from
Equation

4
.
Since
V
diagonalizes
A
,
B
,
D
and
R
, when
Q\in\Theta_{V}
, applying a change of basis using
V
decomposes our multi-dimensional
H_{\infty}
-robustness problem into a collection of one-dimensional problems that admit closed-form solutions.
While these closed-form solutions do not necessarily agree with
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
, alignment regularity (
3
) ensures that there exists an open subset of
\Theta_{V}
along which at least one of the closed-form solutions does agree with
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
.
By explicitly computing the derivative of an agreeing closed-form solution, we obtain a lower bound on
\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{safe}})
, as required.
∎

4
Experiments

This section corroborates our theory (
Section

3
) via experiments in three settings that span different types of agents and different notions of safety:

(i)
a setting that mirrors our theoretical analysis: learning to control an LDS to maximize a task-dependent quadratic reward while handling a risk of potential disturbances (
Section

4.1
);

(ii)
a setting in which a neural network agent is trained to navigate a simulated quadcopter to a task-dependent location, while avoiding regions marked as high-risk (
Section

4.2
);
and

(iii)
a setting in which an LLM agent (LLaMA-3.2 model
[
73
]
) is fine-tuned to perform tasks in a realistic Customer Relationship Management (CRM) benchmark
[
63
]
, while avoiding situations marked as high-risk and handling events defined as a risk materializing (
Section

4.3
).
Our experiments compare learning to imitate a safe teacher to learning to imitate an unsafe teacher (
i.e.
, a teacher that executes tasks while ignoring safety).
In all three settings, we find that although imitating safe and unsafe teachers on tasks seen in training is similarly straightforward, generalizing across tasks is considerably more difficult with the safe teacher.
Code for reproducing our experiments will be made available at
https://github.com/Tomerslortau/agentic-safety-generalization
.

4.1
Linear-Quadratic Control with
H_{\infty}
-Robustness

Our theoretical analysis (
Section

3
) pertains to what is perhaps the simplest embodiment of learning a multi-task agent with safety requirements: learning to control an LDS to maximize a task-dependent quadratic reward, while handling a risk of potential disturbances,
i.e.
, while ensuring
H_{\infty}
-robustness.
In the analyzed setting (
Section

2.3
), generalization across tasks with and without safety requirements reduces to generalization in learning certain target mappings
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
and
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
, respectively.
Our main theoretical result (
Theorem

1
) establishes that, under technical conditions (
1
,
2
, and
3
), the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is greater than or equal to that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
times a particular coefficient which can be arbitrarily large.

Figure

1
corroborates this result by demonstrating empirically that the estimated Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is consistently greater than that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
, even when the coefficient is smaller than one or the technical conditions are violated.

Table

1
provides further corroboration by demonstrating empirically that—in line with the literature
[
19
,
12
,
46
,
108
,
115
,
121
,
89
,
97
]
tying the Lipschitz constant of a target mapping to the difficulty of generalizing when learning it—the separation between Lipschitz constants translates to a separation between cross-task generalization errors.

4.2
Quadcopter Navigation

We demonstrate empirically that the conclusions of our theory (
Section

3
) extend to a setting in which a neural network agent is trained to navigate a simulated quadcopter to a task-dependent location, while avoiding regions marked as high-risk.
This setting, implemented using a differentiable rigid-body simulator
[
86
]
, is a special case of the formulation in
Section

2.2
, characterized as follows.
A state
{\mathbf{x}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{X}}=\mathbb{R}^{12}
represents the quadcopter’s position and tilt angles, along with their respective velocities.
The initial state
{\mathbf{x}}_{0}\in{\mathcal{X}}
is set to zero with the quadcopter hovering.
There is full observability, meaning that observations are equal to their concurrent states (
i.e.
,
{\mathcal{O}}={\mathcal{X}}
and
f_{\text{obs}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},t)={\mathbf{x}}_{t}
).
An action
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}\in{\mathcal{U}}=\mathbb{R}^{4}
represents the thrusts applied to the quadcopter’s rotors.
The task space
\Theta
consists of target states
\theta\in{\mathcal{X}}
in which the components corresponding to tilt angles and velocities are zero (
i.e.
, only the component corresponding to position varies).
The reward is a negative squared Euclidean distance between the concurrent state
{\mathbf{x}}_{t}
and the target state
\theta
(
i.e.
,
f_{\text{reward}}({\mathbf{x}}_{t},{\mathbf{o}}_{t},{\mathbf{u}}_{t},t;\theta)=-\mathopen{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\lVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}{{\mathbf{x}}_{t}-\theta}\mathclose{{\mathchoice{\hbox{$\displaystyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\textstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\left\rVert\vbox to0.0pt{}\right.$}}}}^{2}
).
Safety is captured via risk avoidance, where the set of banned states
{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{ban}}\subseteq{\mathcal{X}}
consists of all those whose position component lies in a fixed, randomly selected union of cubes.
The agent’s policy function
\pi_{\phi}(\cdot)
is implemented by a three-layer fully connected neural network.
The teacher policy function is either
\pi_{\phi_{\mathrm{unsafe}}}(\cdot)
or
\pi_{\phi_{\mathrm{safe}}}(\cdot)
, where the former steers toward the target state while ignoring
{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{ban}}
(
i.e.
, it may lead the state to enter
{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{ban}}
), whereas the latter steers toward the target state while making sure to avoid
{\mathcal{X}}_{\text{ban}}
.
Further details concerning the setting can be found in
Section

G.3
.

Table

1
reports results of the experiment.
In line with our theory, it shows that although imitating
\pi_{\phi_{\mathrm{safe}}}(\cdot)
and
\pi_{\phi_{\mathrm{unsafe}}}(\cdot)
for target states seen in training is similarly straightforward, generalizing across target states is considerably more difficult with
\pi_{\phi_{\mathrm{safe}}}(\cdot)
.

Figure 1:

2.3
), the mapping from task specification to an optimal controller has higher Lipschitz constant with safety requirements than without,
i.e.
, the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is greater than that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
.
Left plot compares the estimated value of the Lipschitz ratio
\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{safe}})/\text{Lip}(K_{\mathrm{unsafe}})
to its lower bound established by
Theorem

1
, when
1
,

, and
3
are met.
For this plot the state, input, noise and action reward matrices
A
,

,
D
and

, respectively, are drawn from a random distribution that ensures:

(i)

1
,

, and
3
are met;
and

Theorem

1
,
i.e.
,
\|A\|_{2}
, varies, while the remaining terms (
\|B\|_{2}
,

and
\alpha_{\mathrm{align}}
) are fixed.
Right plot differs from left in that the random distribution it uses does not ensure

.

4.3
CRM via LLM Agent

We demonstrate empirically that the conclusions of our theory (
Section

3
) extend to a modern agentic setting in which an LLM agent (LLaMA-3.2 model
[
73
]
) is fine-tuned to perform data-handling and record-creation tasks in a realistic CRM benchmark (adaptation of
ST-WebAgentBench

[
63
]
), while avoiding situations marked as high-risk and handling events defined as a risk materializing.
This setting, implemented using a text-based simulated web browser, is a special case of the formulation in
Section

2.2
, characterized as follows.
An observation
{\mathbf{o}}_{t}
represents the current browser page (in AXTree format).
An action
{\mathbf{u}}_{t}
is a token sequence representing a user command (
e.g.
, a click of a button or a text input).
The task space
\Theta
consists of natural language descriptions of different CRM task templates (
e.g.
, “create an account under the name [NAME] with the email [EMAIL]”).
The reward is a binary score which is returned when the action is a terminal command, and indicates whether the specified task was completed successfully.
The agent’s policy function is implemented by a pretrained LLaMA-3.2-1B-Instruct model
[
73
]
, which receives in its context window the CRM task description, the current browser page, the past browser pages, and the past user commands.
Safety is captured via standard CRM requirements that cover both risk avoidance (
e.g.
, avoiding unmonitored high-risk actions such as saving personal information without notifying the user) and risk handling (
e.g.
, detecting materialized input risks such as invalid emails and warning the user before proceeding).
The teacher policy function is implemented by GPT-5.2
[
82
]
, either with safety requirements in its context window (safe teacher) or without (unsafe teacher).
5
5

5

We chose GPT-5.2 for the teacher policy function due to its high quality, and LLaMA-3.2-1B-Instruct for the agent’s policy function since it is open source.
These choices introduce a slight deviation from the formulation in
Section

2.2
, where both policy functions are implemented by the same parametric mapping.

Further details concerning the setting can be found in
Section

G.4
.

Table

1
reports results of the experiment.
In line with our theory, it shows that although imitating safe and unsafe teachers on CRM tasks seen in training is similarly straightforward, generalizing across CRM tasks is considerably more difficult with the safe teacher.

5
Limitations

It is important to acknowledge several limitations of our work.
First, we consider incorporating safety into a learned agent only via imitation learning, and while this approach is common, there are other approaches that we do not account for,
e.g.
, those based on direct interaction with an environment.
Second, our theoretical analysis is restricted to linear-quadratic control, and as a notion of safety it considers only risk handling, not risk avoidance.
Third, the theoretically analyzed linear-quadratic control setting includes as its task parameter
\theta
only the state reward matrix
Q
, not the action reward matrix
R
(the latter is regarded as fixed).
Fourth, our theory considers training in the infinite sample limit, and while this ensures that the established difficulty of safety to generalize across tasks is due to inherent properties of safety, it leaves finite sample effects theoretically unexplored.
Fifth, our theoretical results rely on a stability margin condition (
1
), and, in the case of
Theorem

1
, on a symmetric commutativity condition (
2
) and a mild alignment regularity condition (
3
).
Sixth, rather than directly characterizing generalization across tasks, our theoretical results use the Lipschitz constant as a surrogate, relying on existing literature to bridge the gap.
Seventh, there are cases where the inequality established by
Theorem

1
(
Equation

7
) does not guarantee that the Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is greater than that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
.

Our experiments go beyond most of the theoretical limitations above.
Indeed,
Section

4.1
demonstrates that in the theoretically analyzed linear-quadratic control setting, the estimated Lipschitz constant of
K_{\mathrm{safe}}(\cdot)
is consistently greater than that of
K_{\mathrm{unsafe}}(\cdot)
, even when the conditions of
Theorem

1
(
2
,
1
, and
3
) are violated.

Section

4.1
also demonstrates that the separation between Lipschitz constants translates to a separation between cross-task generalization errors, whether training occurs in the infinite sample limit or in the finite sample regime.
Finally,
Sections

4.2
and
4.3
demonstrate that the separation between generalization errors extends to settings beyond linear-quadratic control, with risk avoidance in addition to risk handling.
Extending our theoretical analysis to match the generality of these empirical demonstrations is an important direction for future work.

6
Related Work

Multi-task agentic settings have been studied in RL and control theory, from early works on hierarchical and task-conditioned decision-making
[
28
,
106
,
102
,
31
,
14
]
, to deep learning-era works on meta-RL
[
35
,
113
,
37
,
91
,
130
,
123
]
.
Recently, multi-task agentic settings have been studied with transformers and LLMs, where an agent receives task specifications and environment observations in its context window, and produces actions via token sequences
[
72
,
62
,
96
,
52
,
99
,
109
,
110
,
112
,
30
,
128
,
67
,
74
,
118
,
90
,
63
,
68
,
92
]
.

Safety is an important pillar of RL and control theory
[
127
,
77
,
80
,
13
,
65
,
38
,
2
,
27
,
105
,
93
,
3
,
71
,
124
,
69
,
116
,
42
,
57
,
22
,
107
,
56
,
43
,
132
,
117
,
79
]
, where it is often studied through the notions of risk avoidance and risk handling: risk avoidance is typically formulated as constraint satisfaction
[
2
,
27
,
105
,
93
,
3
,
71
,
57
,
22
,
56
,
117
,
79
]
, whereas risk handling is typically formulated as robustness to model mismatch and adversarial attacks
[
127
,
77
,
80
,
13
,
65
,
124
,
69
,
42
,
107
,
43
,
132
]
.
Safety is also central to LLM agents
[
39
,
26
,
63
,
29
,
125
,
122
,
44
]
, where risk avoidance is often formulated via prohibited actions
[
39
,
9
,
8
,
26
,
126
,
122
]
, and risk handling is often formulated via requirements to appropriately respond to risky interactions with humans or tools
[
63
,
29
,
125
,
44
]
.
Incorporating safety into a learned agent is often approached via imitation learning
[
8
,
84
,
26
,
103
]
, but there are other approaches as well,
e.g.
, ones based on direct interaction with an environment
[
2
,
27
,
119
,
22
]
.

Generalization across tasks is a prominent area of study in multi-task agentic AI
[
85
,
24
,
23
,
98
,
68
]
.
Mounting empirical evidence suggests that, in RL and control theory as well as with LLM agents, safe behavior often fails to generalize across tasks, even in cases where the agent’s ability to execute does generalize
[
55
,
94
,
78
,
6
,
129
,
61
]
.
To our knowledge, this paper is the first to provide theoretical support for such empirical evidence: it establishes a formal sense in which
generalization across tasks is fundamentally more difficult with safety requirements than without, regardless of how well safety requirements are met on tasks seen in training
.

7
Conclusion

AI agents are increasingly deployed in multi-task settings, where the task to be performed is specified only at test time, and successful deployment hinges on generalization to previously unseen tasks.
Empirical evidence suggests that in such settings, even when the ability to execute generalizes to unseen tasks, the ability to do so safely frequently does not.
This paper provides theory and experiments indicating that failures of agentic safety to generalize across tasks are not merely due to limitations of current training techniques, but reflect a fundamental property of safety itself: the relationship between a task and its safe execution is inherently more complex than the relationship between a task and its execution alone.

Our findings carry two important practical implications:

(i)
generalization to unseen tasks of an agent’s ability to execute does not imply the same for its ability to do so safely;
and

(ii)
the ability to safely execute tasks seen in training, even when it extends to unseen trajectories, does not imply the same for unseen tasks.
Cross-task generalization of safety should therefore be treated as a distinct problem in its own right, separate from cross-task generalization of execution ability and from in-task generalization of safety.

Future work should address the limitations discussed in
Section

5
.
More broadly, we believe that current efforts to enhance agentic safety through more training on a subset of tasks are insufficient, and it is essential to develop fundamentally different approaches.
A promising possibility suggested by our findings is to learn representations for task specifications and safety characteristics which simplify the relationship between them.
We hope that our work will spur progress along this line.
